ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e505	At the request of the Health and Welfare Committee, the President will organise the debate as follows: 3 minutes for each party group and 3 minutes for members of the Government. ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e512	The case concerns the establishment and use of a corona certificate and consent to the inclusion of a regulation on a corona certificate. While large sections of the Committee support the government’s legislative proposal, the Centre Party and the SV have alternative proposals that they themselves will present. As the infection pressure decreases and more is protected, both the public and the business community have a perfectly legitimate expectation of facilitating infectious measures as soon as possible, and the use of corona certificates can also facilitate a faster reopening of society. The Committee and the Government have also proposed that the certificate should contain evidence of vaccination status, disease or negative test result, but when a negative test result is to be equivalent to protection, further consideration is required. With this proposal, the Government will have a regulatory body to determine when the certificate can be used. It is most nearby to relax on number limits and on entry. It is important to note here that the certificate is to be used to provide benefits, not to set restrictions. This means that you can also use this certificate to open things that would otherwise have been closed, not to reduce access to, for example. shops, hairdressers or public offices. These rules shall apply on a temporary basis. The government proposes a maximum of six months, but has also taken the view that the rules on the use of a corona certificate will be terminated in the past if the disease situation would indicate that it is no longer necessary. I would also like to comment briefly on the proposal from the Labour Party, the Advance Party, the Centre Party and SV in the setting. We all agree that persons who are protected should not be at quarantine hotels before entry. Therefore, prior to the referral to the Committee, the government had found a temporary solution in order to ensure, first and foremost, that those vaccinated in Norway, where we have a safe and verifiable system through healthnorge.no, release quarantine hotels. I understand that people who have been vaccinated in countries other than Norway are impatient, but here it is important to stress that we need to have a secure and verifiable system that does not make it easy to cheat when they enter. Then we had also risked that it was easier to get import infection, and we don't want it now. That is why we believe from the government parties that the proposal promoted by these parties is in fact already under follow-up.
ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e531	The Labour Party is behind the government in the case of Korona certificates – certificates to provide benefits and not restrictions on people’s lives. However, we would stress that inertia in the vaccination of the population could help to reinforce the negative and unfair effects of the corona certificate by providing a loss experience for those at the back of the queue. In the worst case, this will also negatively affect motivation and good spirit and put priority under greater pressure. We believe that requiring negative testing as an alternative to domestic vaccination could compensate for some of these challenges, and that it will increase the confidence and legitimacy of using a corona certificate. We need to keep the vaccination rate up and ensure that as much as possible across the country gets the first vaccine within a few weeks. This is primarily about people's lives and health where they live, and we see that outbreaks occur in areas of the country where vaccination is slow. In that case, the vaccine passport means little, but the degree of vaccination is very high. Let us not forget the most important job – to get the most vaccinated, preferably.
ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e540	In the proposal, one requests the consent of the Storting to participate in the decision of the EEA Committee on the inclusion in the EEA Agreement of the Regulation on the Korona Certificate. Together with a Schengen Regulation, it shall form the EU’s common framework for the certificate of Corona. There are several reasons for skeptical consent to include the relevant EU Regulations in the EEA Agreement. The relevant EU legislation is not based on the protection of infectious or public health, but on the free movement of persons between EU and EEA countries. The centre party argued that the adoption of the Korona Certificate Regulations does not in any way limit Norway’s access to take the necessary measures to combat the pandemic. It is also unfortunate that the Storting should consent to the inclusion of EU legislation before the EEA Committee has considered it. The centre party will support the inclusion of the EU Regulation in order to ensure that during the pandemic it will be useful and necessary for the Nordics who will travel to other EEA countries. It is also important that there be a temporary arrangement. But when it comes to the introduction of a national corona certificate, the Centre Party is very sceptical about the government's proposal. Regulating entry and exit to Norway on the basis of information on vaccination, disease or negative testing is quite different from regulating access to activity in Norway. The government's proposal for a corona certificate for domestic use is also not linked to the infection situation or pandemic management, but is proposed for business start-ups. The government's proposal for a national corona certificate is linked to major ethical dilemmas. It is discriminatory on the basis of health information. Of course, this may have a positive impact on business, culture, sport, etc., but it raises questions about how this can be practised while ensuring equal treatment and preventing discrimination as a central principle. There is also a risk that it may be perceived as a general obligation to legitimise. The central party believes that the government has proposed far too wide a mandate for the use of a corona certificate. That proposal cannot be supported by the Centre Party. So we're making an alternative proposal with a narrowing down of what such a national corona certificate can be used for. Among other things, access to public services, educational institutions, shops, shopping centres, training centres, health services or personal care services cannot be conditional on access to them, and it cannot be used in private homes, and it must never be used in the workplace. This proposal makes us an alternative. If that proposal falls, the Central Party does not support the proposal to amend the Code on Corona Certificates. With that, I'll take up the proposal of the Center Party.

ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e593	The revival of society must be fair, and we must make important decisions with a good democratic basis. When the government applies for a certificate without the legislative proposal containing what it should be used in concrete terms – whether or not infectious measures should be taken in relation to persons holding it – it involves far too wide a mandate. Red agrees with the lawyers who recommend that legal regulation should be made far more comprehensive and that the law must specify, as a minimum, the specific purpose to which such a certificate may have rights or facilitate. This can be of great importance to people's ability to participate or not in society. When the pandemic has led to great uncertainty in people's lives, it is all the more important with a good framework for what such a certificate implies, and what it can and cannot be used for. There is a difference between regulating entry and exit based on information on vaccination, disease and negative testing and regulating access to domestic activities. We are going to vote in favour of the alternative legislative proposals from the Centre Party and SV over the government because it is designed with too wide powers and without the purpose of the certificate being defined and defined to a large extent. We do not agree that a certificate can be required for general daily activities that should be open to anyone who is not in isolation or quarantine, for example. access to public services, to education, to shops and to public opinions. In his response to the hearing, the LO also said it was completely wrong if the certificate had any significance for employment. Red agrees. The law must be clear on that, too. Then I am for a safety valve where a third of the minority in Stortinget can stop parts of a regulation. It is particularly important since the government's legislative proposals with too wide powers seem to have a majority.
ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e604	The legislative proposal shall establish a system of safe and verifiable documentation of vaccination status, undergo coronary disease and test results, and provide general rules for the use of a corona certificate. The Government has also requested the consent of the Stortinge to participate in the decision of the EEA Committee on the inclusion in the EEA Agreement of the EU Regulation on Korona Certificate. This Regulation will establish a common framework for the issue, verification and acceptance of a Corona certificate in Europe. The Korona certificate could contribute to a gradual and controlled reopening of society. The population has an expectation of relief from quarantine and other burdensome measures for the protection of infectious diseases in line with the number of vaccinated persons. Similarly, business needs to get wheels started as quickly as possible. This is desirable because it reduces both the personal and financial adverse effects of the pandemic. It is important for me to make it clear that Korona licences are to be used domestically to provide benefits, not to impose restrictions. It is proposed that the Ministry be empowered to provide for evidence of corona status in order to obtain relief from the covid-19 regulation. Assessments as to the availability of differentiations in the protection of infection must be made on the basis of existing legislation of the Code of Disease Protection and temporary law on restrictions on the entry of non-residents for reasons of public health. It is proposed that the Norwegian legislation should apply for six months because the need is temporary and EU legislation should be time-limited. The Korona certificate must work both within and outside our borders. As long as EU legislation exists, Norwegian citizens will be able to benefit from the Korona certificate when travelling to other EEA countries. On 20 May, the European Parliament and the Member States concluded an agreement on the content of the Regulation. The agreement involves certain amendments to the proposal attached to the proposal, but not for fundamental reasons, such as determining its use by national authorities. This Regulation has not been published and final but is intended to apply in the EU during June 2021. The draft decision of the EEA/EFTA States to the EEA Committee is under consideration in the EU bodies. The consent of the majority to participate in the decision of the EEA Committee is necessary in accordance with the second paragraph of Section 26 of the Constitution. Legislative amendment proposals relating to the EU rules on Korona Certificate cannot enter into force until the EEA Regulation has been adopted and incorporated into the EEA Agreement. Such a decision cannot take place until after the adoption of the Regulation in the EU and has therefore not yet been taken. In order to save time and ensure legal entity throughout the EEA, it is important that the decision can enter into force simultaneously for the EEA/EFTA countries, or as soon as possible after the Regulation for application in the EU Member States. The Government will return to the Storting should substantial changes be decided on in the draft EEA Committee decision and Regulation.

ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e735	Earlier this year, we dealt with the climate communication, which was supposed to be the road map to achieve our climate targets. Only today do we deal with the question of what goals it is. The EU, on its side, with which we have committed ourselves to working together to achieve the climate objectives, has launched a comprehensive process that means possible changes to all the legislation that affect how emissions are calculated and accounted for in the agreement we have together. So we have no idea, a little bit, where we're going, or how we're going to get there. The centre party regrets that the process has been reversed. It makes it difficult to make good decisions that will actually take us to the objectives we are obliged to achieve in the Paris Agreement. We're not alone in thinking that. The NHO is one of the hearing bodies that points out this precisely, and believes that the Storting should first address the outcome of the EU climate target process before changing the climate law and setting new percentage targets. The Norwegian climate debate has the mark of being a target debate, a competition to set the highest possible goals, but it is a little constructive competition, which does not bring us forward. It is important to remember that the Paris Agreement points to a balance between anthropogenic emissions and greenhouse gas uptake. So we need a large-scale commitment to carbon-negative solutions. For the Central Party, it is therefore essential to know how the forest and land use legislation becomes for Norway and how it will affect the use of our natural resources. What we need is a regulatory framework that ensures that we can continue to make use of nature and maximise carbon uptake in soil and forest and go from non-renewable production of goods to renewable production while contributing to increased carbon capture. The fundamental problem in this context is that the objectives we have committed ourselves to through the EU are carbon neutrality, i.e. emissions are no greater than carbon capture. How can this be linked when the government does not propose solutions that include bonds or set binding targets? We need climate-negative measures, and an example of this is the wood pulp producers Norske Skog Saugbruks in Halden and Borregaard in Sarpsborg. They have reduced their own emissions to close to zero, produce climate-neutral products and would be climate-negative with a capture and storage facility at Borg Havn. On the other hand, the government has no strategy to stimulate that type of activity, product or plan, and it is quite alarming. All in all, this suggests that the Storting should work more on the content, wait for what will become the current legislation in this area, and in the meantime work to achieve the goals already set. Furthermore, we think it is a good idea that the Stortinget debate the objectives annually, as the Labour Party has proposed.

ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e1477	At the request of the Labour and Social Committee, the President will organise the debate as follows: 5 minutes for each party group and 5 minutes for members of the Government. Moreover, within the allocated speaking time, there will be up to five lines with replies from members of the government, and those who have signed up to the allocated speaking time will have a speaking time of up to 3 minutes.
ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e1484	In this case, the government is promoting proposals for amendments to the Act on Age Limits for Public Servants and to the Law on the Pension Scheme for Nurses. The proposal involves removing the obligation to leave at the acquired special age limit. It is assumed that the changes will also have an impact on the municipal sector. The government's proposal means that those employed in posts with a special age limit of 60 years, 63 years or 65 years of age should be able to choose to continue in the post themselves until they reach the general age limit in the public sector, which is now 70 years old. At the same time, the government indicates that they have a proposal for the Grand Duchy to increase the general age limit to 72 years. The Government Parties and the Support Party The Advance Party anticipates the government's work and makes concrete proposals to increase the age limit to 72 in this case. At the same time, the Government Parties and the Advance Party ask that the Government also consider a further increase in the age limits in working life to 75 years, which shall also apply to employees currently subject to special age limits. This means that the obligation to leave at the acquired special age limit in the future will not be lifted until the age of 70, but until the age of 72 and perhaps thereafter until 75. The current rules on retirement at the special age limit at 60 years, 63 years and 65 years respectively are set out in Section 2 of the Age Limits Act and relate to posts where the service imposes exceptional physical or mental strain on employees or where the service imposes special requirements on physical or mental characteristics. With the government's proposal, this means that employees with a special age limit can remain in office for up to 10 years after what is currently set as a special age limit. With the announcement from the government that a case is being prepared to increase the general age limit to 72 years, we are talking about 12 years, and with the proposal from the government parties and the Progress Party in 75 years, we are talking about up to 15 years beyond what is currently being set as a special age limit. The Committee has been consulted on the matter. Twelve organisations organising special age-limited employees participated in the hearing, none from the employer’s side. With the exception of the academics, all the hearing bodies strongly opposed the government's proposal in the case. There were two main reasons for this. It was referred to the agreement between the government and the employee organisations that amendments to the rules on special age limits should be the subject of negotiations between the parties to the cooling water of the new occupational pension agreement for public sector employees. Negotiations should focus on a permanent pension scheme for persons with a special age limit, which is in practice without the possibility of compensating for the adjustment of their life expectancy, and then on who in the future should be covered by the special age limit rules and what the special age limits should actually be for those to be covered. Organisations underlined the justification for today's special age limit rules, which are three-fold. More than 200000 workers are currently covered by these provisions. They have a very demanding working day. Examples of special age workers include fire and rescue workers, care and care workers, police, prison services, defence, sanitation and health services. The loosing association was also present during the hearing. These are workers with a very demanding working day and with special requirements for suitability. Organisations fear that their more favourable pension rights, justified by the obligation to leave, may actually lapse when that obligation is lifted. The second reason was for employers, who could risk having an increasing number of workers requiring special organisation without the need for tasks. The third reason was simply the risk of impaired social security and preparedness. The majority of the Committee, which is thus the minority in the Grand Duchy, has chosen to take account of the recordings at the hearing and to state that the matter is being referred back to the Government and that the parties are at the negotiating table. The minority of the Committee, which is the majority in the Chamber, has chosen the opposite, to support the government's proposal to abolish the obligation to resign at the level of special age and, at the same time, to propose to extend the age limit further to perhaps up to 75 years. The Labour Party believes that the views of the Government Parties and the Advance Party on this matter are both irresponsible in terms of social security and preparedness and show a very serious breach of the rules of the labour market. Unfortunately, the minority's proposal is adopted by the Storting later today, but if the government changes, we shall make sure to change that matter as quickly as possible.
ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e1501	Societal developments have led to a high level of adaptation of the rules on special age limits to today’s society. We are expected to live longer, and therefore it is natural to assume that we are also able to work at an older age than before. Family supervisor Kari Grönås and midwife Eli Ljosnes would like to continue working when they were 65 years old, but because of their age, the municipality said they had to stop. Both wanted to work a few more years. Health worker Kim Pilgaard wrote in 2018 in Fagbladet that he is struggling to understand why those who are not struggling are to be punished for what they deserve. To him it felt like a punishment, perhaps also age discrimination, and he earnestly asked the trade unions to take care of those who would like to work longer. He was also critical that the union had given the employer almost unlimited power to fire him for no reason. Kari, Eli and Kim are not alone. Almost every third employee in the public sector is subject to special age limits which force them into retirement when they reach the age of 60, 63 or 65. Many of these have a strong desire to work longer. Unfortunately, age limits are limited. The obligation to leave at the special age limit is over-mature to be removed. Society has been changing considerably since the special age limits were set several decades ago. In today's society, people are healthier and live longer, fewer children are born, and society needs more hands in work. Then it's backwards to force people who both can and will work out of work. What we're doing now is just saying that you can get rid of it at a special age, but if you want to keep going, you should also be allowed to do it. Eliminating the obligation to leave is an important step towards an age-independent job protection. Modifying the obligation to withdraw from a right of withdrawal, while still retaining the right to a special age pension, is in line with both the pension reform and the working line, and it gives the employees extended rights. With this proposition, we state that suitability in position should be linked to qualifications and abilities rather than years of birth. Several hearing bodies have pointed out that this could lead to increased quality of life and better health, as well as labour income and increased service provision. Yes, it actually means a lot to us to be able to use our skills and competences, and the job is an important part of our lives. Age limits have a normative force and affect the general perception of the appropriate age of departure. This hampers the working line and may lead to shortages of labour in several professions. In the districts are almost all fire constables part-time, with full work next door. Is it reasonable that these should end when they reach the age of 60, even though the municipality may not have enough candidates for the positions? Should not the smoke diving test and other qualification requirements be decisive and not what year you were born? Age in itself cannot be an obstacle. Then we lose valuable skills in working life, several years before what is common to other occupational groups. We also need more midwives and nurses. At that time, there must be political will to maintain this important competence in the labour market for as long as possible. In the debate, one often brings out those who, for some reason, are unable to work because of age, but who do not realize this for themselves. Is it right to exclude the vast majority of healthy elderly people who want to continue working? No, of course not. There must be good human resources policies at work, where good dialogue takes care of employees throughout the work cycle, and senior policy is an essential part of this. As early as 2013, the then Federal Head of Police Union, Sigve Bolstad, called for the ‘good senior policy’ and said the following: ‘We need to remove the obstacles that mean that those who want to continue for more than 60 years must go canoeing and apply to continue, instead of being in the workplace, to stand in the position of resigning.’ He further argued that priority should be given to maintaining valuable skills and making it tempting to extend the professional career. With this proposition, we are giving thousands of workers today an important right and the possibility of a decent departure from work when you want it. We need the labour we can get – and then it should be lacking that we politicians do not give workers the opportunity to continue, if that is what they themselves want. With that, I'm going to deal with the suggestions that we're part of.

ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e1732	At the request of the Labour and Social Committee, the President will organise the debate as follows: 3 minutes for each party group and 3 minutes for members of the Government. ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e1739	Today, we are taking further steps in the fight for a serious, safe and secure working life. This proposal proposes changes to the penal code, the labour environment law, the compulsory occupational pension law and the Public Service Act, which will contribute in particular to the reinforced criminal law and administrative follow-up to the crime of working life. I would like to thank the Committee for its good cooperation and now present the Committee's position. Strong tools are needed to address the crime of working life, and the Committee welcomes new tools to combat it. The changes presented in the proposal give clear signals of the harmfulness of serious work-life crime and its serious consequences for workers, businesses and society. Exploitation of foreign workers and the avoidance of taxes are two of the most common forms of labour crime. Immigrants are more vulnerable than Norwegian workers because they are often willing to accept poorer pay and working conditions. Ecocrim demonstrates in its threat assessment that this can lead to considerable savings in labour-intensive professions and projects where many hours of work are laid down, which is also distorting competition. Extensive use of cash is also a characteristic of criminal actors, typically by facilitating black trading. The proposal promotes a self-punishment against theft of wages and salaries with an upper penalty of six years in prison. This provision is further incorporated in the criminal law in order to highlight the seriousness and priority of the hearing received. Proposals are also made for wages and salaries to be paid through banks as a clear rule, while allowing for a narrow exemption. The proposal also proposes that compulsory occupational retirement provision be penalised, which is also widely supported by the hearing bodies. Compulsory occupational retirement provision must be considered as an important part of the total remuneration and the absence of follow-up of obligations will have serious consequences for the workers affected. The Ministry also promotes clarification of the enforcement competence of the Labour Inspectorate under the Public Service Act. Furthermore, it is proposed to increase the penalties for increased circumstances in the Work Environment Act, from three to five years, up to the same level as the Nature Diversity Act and the Pollution Act. This will make HMS legislation more consistent, and there is strong support among the hearing bodies for this. Both LO and NHO indicate that this will give a clear signal of the seriousness of the violation of the law and that occupational crime is undesirable. Finally, I refer to the proposal for an approval system in the car industry, supported by a majority of the committee. Such a mechanism will strengthen the employment of employees, facilitate the choice of customers by serious actors and help authorities to get an overview of the different actors. With this proposition, we guarantee the rights of workers and take further action to counteract black work and the crime of working life. With that, I also take up the proposals we are part of, and look forward to a good debate.

ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e1807	In 2015, the government launched a strategy against the crime of working life. It has been revised several times, last in February 2021. This proposition follows up several of the measures of the strategy. We propose a new provision in the penal code on punishment for employers as deliberately and for their own gain fails to pay agreed or statutory remuneration or compensation. Theft of wages and salaries will be punishable by fines or prisons for up to 2 years, or for up to 6 years by ‘gross pay theft’. In this way, the government wants to contribute to a better balance between how society treats employers who steal from their employees and workers who steal from their employer. At the same time, the proposal provides the authorities with additional tools to combat the most unprofessional activities. In the extension of the pay theft proposal, further proposals are being made to introduce criminal liability for offences against the compulsory occupational pension law, to clarify in the Public Service Act that the Labour Inspectorate may impose obligations on the payment of public pay, and to strengthen the penal framework of the Labour Environment Act for the most serious offences. The second main proposal in the proposal concerns the introduction of a provision in the Working Environment Act that wages are to be paid via a bank. The proposal implies that, as a clear rule, pay in cash should be prohibited and that the Labour Inspectorate should be empowered to enforce the new legal requirement. The aim is to reduce the scope for action of those benefiting from the crime of working life, reduce the scope of undeclared work and ensure that workers are given greater security for their social security rights. Finally, it is proposed to amend the Working Environment Act to allow for the establishment of a system of approval for establishments providing services in connection with car care, tyre or wheel changes, etc. The authorisation system shall improve working conditions, facilitate customer choice of serious suppliers and help authorities to better understand the different actors. I am pleased that, like the large masses in the consultation round, the Labour and Social Committee is also largely in agreement with the changes.

ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e2050	This case marks an important distinction in the way we think about day money and education. The need to change a rigid regulatory framework that prevents the lay-off and the unemployed from taking the training that may be the way back to work has long been clear. Today’s labour market is increasingly demanding skills and formal qualifications, and it has been a paradox that you cannot train when you get a day’s allowance, while that education could have been the key to getting a new job. There is also a background to three public investigations that have proposed changes to the day-to-day pay legislation and that were established before the coronary apathy further updated this issue. So this is not a new issue. For example, in the years following the fall in the oil price in 2014, we saw problems with the fact that there were too many obstacles for the out-of-work and unemployed who wanted to retrain to get back to work more quickly, leading to more embellishment. But there is also no doubt that the coronary apathy has underlined the need for these changes to an even greater extent, as there are people with shorter education who are hardest hit when people are outlawed and lost their jobs. This led to a need for a temporary scheme that was put in place last April, and now there are major and permanent changes to the rules, which will make it easier for many to get the skills they need to get to work more quickly. These changes now provide in particular for the possibility for beneficiaries to take primary and secondary education and vocational education. For this type of training, a lower age limit of 25 years has been proposed, with the possibility of taking full-time training and limitations on the requirement to be a true jobseeker. For higher education and universities, there are some more limitations, such as: as regards age limit, study progression, duration and requirements to be a true job seeker, and a lower age limit of 30 years. It is important that the general rule continues to be that daily allowances are a temporary security of income and that daily allowances are not initially to become an alternative source of funding for training or education. At the same time, it is important to ensure that people do not have to choose between the daily allowance and the skills they take just to get back to work more quickly. That's why there's an important and joyful principleal distinction we're making today with these changes, which will lead to a more adaptable working life.

The proposition we have to deal with today is a follow-up to Decision No. 319 of 15 December 2017. The decision was taken against the votes of the representatives of the Right, the Party of Advancement and the Left. It was before the Christian People's Party joined the government – and out of Norwegian working life – and it was after the crisis associated with the oil price drop that we promoted that proposal. It must be allowed to say that it has taken a long time. So one crisis was not enough for the right-wing government. It had to be another crisis before it did what the Storting asked for. The working party clearly supports the intention of the proposition. We have promoted this ourselves. If this is to work as intended, it is essential that the unemployed and those on leave are properly informed about the opportunities available to participate in education and that they are closely followed up by Nav. Therefore, we are promoting proposals for decisions to impose on Nav an information obligation and a systematic follow-up of the beneficiaries without completing high school or primary school. The proposal does not mention whether existing study offers will meet the need for more people to combine daily allowances and education in the future. We know that the demand for skilled workers will increase sharply in the years ahead. It requires a general strengthening of both the vocational training system, the apprenticeship system and the vocational training courses, so that there is actually a real offer of study for persons wishing to take advantage of the possibility of combining daily allowances with education. For professional studies, we know that there is already a major shortage of available apprenticeships. It is therefore important that the government follow this closely and assess the need to extend the temporary derogations at a later stage.

ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e2370	There is a vote on proposal No. One from the Center Party. The proposal reads as follows: ‘I In Law No. 5 August 1994 55 on the protection against communicable diseases is amended as follows: [[Infection protection law]] the following Chapter 4A is inserted: Cape. 4A Provisional provisions for the certificate of corona § 4A-1 Korona certificate The Korona certificate shall demonstrate, in accordance with a single European technical framework, that a person has been vaccinated against covid-19, has undergone covid-19 based on an approved test or test result for SARS-CoV-2. The Ministry may provide for the implementation of obligations arising from the EEA Agreement on the EU Korona Certificate. § 4A-2 Domestic use of the Korona certificate The Ministry may, where measures for the protection of infection would otherwise not be proportionate, provide that the measure shall not apply to those who can demonstrate vaccination against covid-19 by means of a corona certificate, undergo covid-19 based on an approved test or negative test for the virus SARS-CoV-2. The provisions on the sole accepted evidence of vaccination status, covid-19 and test results may only be determined where effective and safe verification is deemed necessary, where there is easy access to testing, and where the nature and conditions of the protection measure would not otherwise be disproportionate. A licence shall only be required if it is necessary to establish the identity of the licence holder. The presentation of a Korona certificate may not be made as a condition for access to public services or educational institutions, shops and shopping centres, training centres, or for access to health services or services related to personal care. Nor may it be made a condition for travelling in a public place, or for participating in public demonstrations or other indications of public opinion. Korona certificates cannot be set as a condition for visits to private homes, participation in religious ceremonies and cannot be used in employment. Corona certificate requirements may only be made in the context of the introduction of reliefs in the otherwise applicable disease control measures. No requirement for a Coronal Certificate may be made in the context of the introduction of new measures for the protection of infection. No discrimination may be granted on the grounds that they are unable to produce a corona certificate, unless such access is granted by law or by law. It is not allowed to require evidence in the form of a corona certificate from children under 16 years of age or persons who, for medical reasons or due to disability, are unable to carry out a vaccine or a covid-19 test. For the purposes of public or commercial activities, it shall be prohibited to require a certificate of corona where this does not result from the provisions of law, regulation or administrative action. Infringement of this provision is punished as an infringement under Section 8-1 of the Act. III The law applies from the time the King decides. The king may decide that the individual provisions of the law shall enter into force at different times. Part I of the Act shall be repealed six months after its entry into force.’;» Red has announced support for the proposal.
ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e2425	There is a vote on proposal No. Four, from the Socialist Left Party. The proposal reads as follows: ‘In Law No 5 August 1994 55 on the protection against communicable diseases is amended as follows: new Chapter 4A is replaced by the following: [[Infection protection law]] 4A Temporary provisions on the Korona Certificate § 4A-1 Korona Certificate The Korona Certificate shall, in accordance with a single European technical framework, demonstrate that a person has been vaccinated against covid-19, has undergone covid-19 based on validated test or test results for SARS-CoV-2. The Ministry may provide for the implementation of obligations arising from the EEA Agreement on the EU Korona Certificate. § 4A-2 The use of the corona certificate by the Ministry of the Interior may, where other infectious diseases measures would not be proportionate, provide that the measure shall not apply to those who can demonstrate vaccination against covid-19 by means of a corona certificate, undergo covid-19 based on an approved test or negative test for the virus SARS-CoV-2. The provisions on the sole accepted evidence of vaccination status, covid-19 and test results may only be determined where effective and safe verification is deemed necessary, where there is easy access to testing, and where the nature and conditions of the protection measure would not otherwise be disproportionate. A licence shall only be required if it is necessary to establish the identity of the licence holder. The presentation of a Korona certificate may not be made as a condition for access to public services or educational institutions or shops and shopping centres. Nor may it be made a condition for travelling in a public place, or for participating in public demonstrations or other indications of public opinion. Korona certificates cannot be set as a condition for visits to private homes, participation in religious ceremonies and cannot be used in employment. Corona certificate requirements may only be made in the context of the introduction of reliefs in the otherwise applicable disease control measures. No requirement for a Coronal Certificate may be made in the context of the introduction of new measures for the protection of infection. No discrimination may be granted on the grounds that they are unable to produce a corona certificate, unless such access is granted by law or by law. It is not allowed to require evidence in the form of a corona certificate from children under 16 years of age or persons who, for medical reasons or due to disability, are unable to carry out a vaccine or a covid-19 test. For the purposes of public or commercial activities, it shall be prohibited to require a certificate of corona where this does not result from the provisions of law, regulation or administrative action. Infringement of this provision is punished as an infringement under Section 8-1 of the Act. III The Law applies from the time the King decides. The king may decide that the individual provisions of the law shall enter into force at different times. Part I of the Act shall be repealed six months after its entry into force.’;» The Greens and Reds have given their support to the proposal. The centre party has notified subsidized support for the proposal.

ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e3550	I want to start by thanking the proposers for the matter. In our view, this case raises obvious fundamental issues that it is worth looking at. The matter is therefore the appointment of co- magistrates in the field of employment protection and the arrangement we have with the appointment of co- magistrates in these cases on a proposal from the parties and not on the basis of the principle of chance, which is otherwise the norm in the general courts. The representative’s proposal proposes to replace current practice by a random sample of co-judges from each of the specific labour-intensive selections. The transparency of the specific lifelong committees of judges is an important function. That's why no one wants to change these either. However, we believe, like the proposers, that there is reason to ask questions in the practice where the co-judgments are also drawn on a case-by-case basis on a proposal from the parties to the case. The independence and trust of the courts is one of the main principles of the rule of law. It should therefore not be sufficient to say that we have a system that works well today or to say that we are satisfied only with the constitutional and Conventional obligations. All of this is probably correct. The question should, however, be: are there elements of the current system that are suitable for creating uncertainty as to whether independence is adequately safeguarded? And is there in the extension of the alternatives that can deliver this in a more optimal way? If the answer to both of these questions is yes, it would be irresponsible not to ask for this to be investigated at least. The examples to which the proposers refer in their comments, including the fact that the LO systematically points out judges from its own associations, and that this affects their votes, give cause for concern. There is also reason to ask questions as to whether the parties may be at risk of pointing out their favorite judges at the expense of the rights of unorganised persons. I would also like to stress that our comments and our proposal for a decision on the matter are not due to the fact that we have concluded on these questions. So it may be that there are enough good arguments to keep the current system, and that these concerns do not turn out to be good enough. However, many important arguments have been raised against today's system, and similarity to the law is at least, in our view, too important a principle that one can germinate from this. That is why we do not now support decisions on legislative changes, but we believe that the proposal needs to be examined. With that, I take up the proposal on the right-hand side of the matter.

ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e3603	Employment protection matters are demanding, and the treatment is laid down by law. It has been a long practice, and it is part of the Norwegian model that there is trust between the parties, and that the parties are responsible, knowledgeable and make the necessary adjustments. ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e3612	The purpose of the system is to ensure that the general courts have the necessary knowledge and expertise in the field of occupational safety. That's an important consideration. On the basis of today's rules, the court appoints co-judges in each case on a proposal from the parties to the case. The co-judgments shall assess and rule on the case on an independent basis. It is very important to stress that the rules of the Court of Justice on stability also apply to the labour-intensive co-judgments. So there are the same requirements for objectivity and neutrality for the co-judgments as for other judges. The system of co-judges is well established, and my assessment has been that it works mainly as it should. At the same time, the government believes that it is central that the Court of Justice and the procedures for the appointment of judges have confidence both in the users of the Court and in society in general. As others have pointed out, my answers to the committee have been somewhat more conservative than that we should explore possible alternatives. At the same time it's like conservatively trying to see if there's anything that can be improved, but then it's important to do it in a proper and thorough way, through a good investigation. We have to be clear that there may be improvement points, and I do not exclude that there may be reason to consider whether there are other and better ways to point out which fellow judges are going to serve in each case. At the same time, the matter raises some important fundamental questions. The government believes that we need to have a thorough investigation – we need to have a good dialogue with the social partners – before any proposals for changes to the Working Environment Act should be made in this area, but we naturally follow up the decision promoted by the government parties in Stortinget. Finally, in this case, questions have also been raised about today's system in line with Conventional requirements for the independence of the courts, and there is evidence that Norway has been sued before the Council of Europe's Social Law Committee, claiming a breach of the European Social Charter. The government assumes that today's legislation is consistent with the country's international legal obligations, and then we await the evaluations and conclusion of the social justice committee.

The state instruments, such as the Free Fund, the leisure card and inclusion in the cultural life scheme, will contribute to this, but we also rely on close cooperation with the actors in the field, the municipalities and the county municipalities. Interaction and coordination are central to the best possible provision of art and culture for children and young people. This message is made in close cooperation between the Minister of Knowledge and me – both because the cultural school is a central theme, and also because we work together on a unique scheme, namely the cultural school bag. Because it's an arena where all children and young people can have equal opportunities to experience professional art and culture, it's in school. However, the functioning of the scheme requires equivalent and structured cooperation and interaction between the cultural sector and the education sector. That's why we have the Culture idea, which is going to help make the cultural school bag an even better arrangement for children and young people, for artists and for everyone working with the DKS around the country. In addition, the Communication has highlighted tasks in the field of child and youth culture that need to be addressed. We have made use of funds for digital efforts to contribute to up-to-date tools that communicate on young people’s premises and to a pilot project to integrate children’s and young people’s culture into municipal planning. When we have a staff working with the arts and culture of children and young people, who are in close dialogue with the municipal sector, the county councils, the children's and youth organisations, and not least children and young people themselves, it is also quite natural to use the Culture idea to strengthen the work of children and young people outside the DCS. The Communication also proposes measures to ensure better coordination with regard to the digital life of children and young people. Today, an increasing proportion of the lives of children and young people are on, or integrated with, digital platforms. It is therefore necessary for the authorities to have coordinated efforts to secure our children on these surfaces. We want to achieve this by providing a national strategy for secure digital growth. Children and young people have repeatedly stated that they want to be listened to in the design of the arts and culture offer. A report from the Youth Freedom Council says it is a major challenge that young people do not see themselves represented in art. Art and culture are thus restricted to too few. This is a major problem, because culture is an expression of community-building power, and for young people it should be as natural to express oneself through art as it is for adults. In the Communication, the Government therefore proposes measures to involve children and young people, to be in dialogue with them and to involve them in all parts of cultural life. This should not be at the expense of artistic freedom or the principle of arm's length distance, but should be a measure to ensure that we know the group we're trying to reach. This should be in everyone's interest, because if we are to create an artistic and cultural offer that is relevant and representative of children and young people, we need to talk to them. We do not believe that children and young people should be given what they want at all times. The point is, in order to get there, we have to know their reality, what hits them, and what they're concerned about. The opposition points to a lack of ambition in the message. I think we have quite clear ambitions. We want children and young people to have equal opportunities to experience art and culture. We want children and young people to be listened to. We want the arts and culture offer to be relevant and representative. We aim to improve interaction and coordination. I believe that we need the state actors, we also need the Culture idea, to help achieve the objectives set – of course in cooperation with the municipalities, county councils and the private and voluntary actors. Finally, I would like to say that I am pleased that this message has created so much commitment. It shows that this theme is important for the whole sector. I also hope that this commitment will strengthen the artistic and cultural offer to children and young people and give children and young people the opportunity to experience, create and share.

ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e4193	The cultural school is an important venue for children and young people's opportunities to develop as whole people. It has been exciting to follow the development of the cultural school from its inception as various private activities carried out by local fires, to the flourishing work being done today over the whole country under the auspices of the municipal culture school. In the 1980s it took a new form, and with the impact of the Christian People's Party, there was a state teacher's grant for every teacher's hour the municipalities used for what was then called music school. When the municipalities discovered the importance of the cultural school for the development of children's different cultural interests and abilities, there was a strong desire to expand the offer to include more culture, and in the 1990s we received a subsidy scheme for the municipalities that included several forms of art and culture. Dance environment, drama and visual arts were part of the system. Then also started a completely necessary struggle to legislate an obligation for the municipalities to set up cultural schools with state grants, and during the Bondevik I government in 1997 it became a statutory obligation to establish cultural and music schools. Last, but not least in this context, it has now been four years since the Christian People's Party promoted the proposal for a big message for a strengthened cultural school for the future. I have to say I am very pleased that the government has great ambitions on behalf of and in cooperation with the cultural school to which we are today discussing an important contribution. This message acknowledges the challenge of having equal opportunities wherever you live, and is now taking new steps to ensure that this is the case. The cultural school is an important offer and in many places forms a central part of the cultural offer to children and young people. It is also true that many young people today have unique opportunities to develop their skills and interests in cultural schools that they do not have in other arenas. They have their first meeting with art and culture, with commitment and with mastery faith as an alternative form of communication. The possibility of sharing thoughts and accepting others, the possibility of sharing an art expression and accepting others, is what makes us evolve as whole people, and to make it equal for our children, we need to ensure increased participation and availability. Therefore, we are very pleased that the Council of State has chosen to include in the communication the vision of the Council of Culture Schools – cultural education for all. Now the government has delivered on ambitions and visions for the cultural school. Now the cultural school has to be an offer for everyone.

ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e4825	The objectives of film policy shall take account of several and partial cross-cutting considerations. This is important for the economic sustainability of the film industry, but also for Norwegian filmmakers to deliver quality content to a Norwegian audience. Film funding is increasingly international. Some sources of funding, in particular incentive schemes and regional funds, require admission in the country concerned. For co-productions too, admissions are normally required in the countries participating in the funding. At the same time, limiting Norwegian producers’ opportunities to international financing would limit Norwegian producers’ ability to finance their productions. I understand that the proposers do not specify or at all suggest anything about what will be a reasonable spending requirement. Indeed, it would be difficult to find a balanced level of any capital requirement. An excessive requirement is likely to be negative, in particular for productions with international ambitions, and in worst cases make these productions impossible to realise. At the same time, too mild a requirement would have little or no effect on the proportion of funds used in Norway and limited impact on the number of recording days made within borders. In addition, the proposers say little about how Norwegian producers will replace falling funding if they turn back the crane for international financing. There is a significant risk that a slightly flexible and well-thought-out demand for consumption in Norway will be counterproductive for Norwegian film production. This is confirmed by Norwegian producers, who have warned against introducing a so-called capital requirement and instead pointed to instruments such as the incentive scheme. I point out that the government is proposing to strengthen the stipulations in the revised national budget with a total framework of 100 million. NOK, and we come back with a proposal for a long-term arrangement of the government budget for 2022. ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e4930	Thank you to the Cooperation Committee on this matter, where the Socialist Left Party, supported by the Workers' Party, supports proposals for strong and independent cultural governance, including: by examining and modifying the roles and functions of the Culture Council and the Culture Tank, by strengthening Norwegian Cultural Fund considerably, and by examining and amending the Culture Act. A total of five proposals are presented by the two parties. Although the rest of the Committee does not support the proposals, it is an overall committee which underlines the importance of strong and independent cultural management where the principle of arm's length is central and where the role of the State is to facilitate a rich and varied cultural life. The Committee is also jointly concerned that cultural policy should focus on values such as diversity, innovation and high artistic and cultural quality. We in the Center Party believe that the process of these movements has been bad, and we believe the Ministry of Culture can largely handle the tasks they have had until this move. We don't like the cultural council becoming more and more like a directorate, which we as a party are not interested in getting in the cultural field as well. There are really no more directorates and additional bureaucracy Norway needs — nor do they need fewer. Our strong opinion on this also contributes to the fact that we do not support several of the proposals that have been promoted today, even though we might be interested in supporting parts of their content. The proposers are so clear that, unlike us, they see a new directorate. But let it be said: we in the Center Party are definitely critical of the Culture Tank and want an investigation, if not in the direction of the directorate that the proposal provides. We choose otherwise to wait to make changes to the Culture Act, which is one of the proposals, until it is discussed later, in a revision of it. To the proposal to increase the Culture Fund considerably: it will be more right for the Centre Party to take it into account when working on the budget, even if we are absolutely in order to increase the framework of the Culture Fund. ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e4947	Norway has strong and unique cultural governance. Since its establishment, the Norwegian Cultural Council has been enlarged and extended its mandate, strengthening Norwegian cultural life. The independence of the Council of Culture, the principle of arm's length distance and decisions taken in the field of art and culture ensure a strong, diverse and independent cultural life. But in recent years a cultural life has been seen under pressure and a situation in which the Cultural Council has had to cut allocations. At the same time, a number of new schemes and establishments have been put in place without it being clear how they relate to the existing cultural management. The cultural idea is the great example, but there is also a mention of the commitment to culture as a nourishment and the gift-enhancing system. The government has done reorganisations in the last year, especially with the Council of Culture, without proper anchoring and proper debate, either in cooperation with the field or here in the Stortinget. The need for a clean-up in particular roles and the Council of Culture has been there for a long time and has been discussed in the past, but now is the time. The ongoing reorganisation of the government should be halted until a proper investigation has been carried out and a proper debate has taken place on the organisation of cultural management as a whole. The SV's objectives for such an investigation and such a debate have several sides. On the one hand – perhaps most importantly – we want an even stronger and more independent cultural council, and we want to strengthen transparency, power distribution and the arm's length principle. On the other hand – which is also important – we will have an even richer cultural offer across the country through stronger municipal and state cultural governance, which can give the cultural field more power in the meeting with and in competition with other well-organised sectors. A possible distinction within the Council of Culture, with the arm's length distance tasks one place and the directorate tasks another, may be the solution. It must be seen in the context of the rest of cultural management, as an alternative organisation of the cultural school system, where the funds go more to the provision of children and young people and to a lesser extent to bureaucracy, or a strengthened cultural law, which also imposes requirements on municipal cultural management. The SV do not point with these proposals to the fait accompli, but we point to the need for debate and investigation. I note that the debate has been going quite well in the last few weeks. It's very joyful, because both the field and the Greatness need to be included in a real, new and comprehensive investigation and process to strengthen our cultural policy infrastructure, both nationally and locally. I'm addressing the suggestions SV is part of.

ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e4967	Art must be free. This is ensured by the management of state resources for art and culture in accordance with the principle of arm's length. The distance principle of arms length shall ensure a necessary distance from political interests and that no organised interests affect the exercise of free artistic and cultural discretion. Representatives of Andersen, Fagerås and Hövstegård believe that there is a need for a number of new studies of cultural management, in order to strengthen the principle of arm’s length distance, strengthen the directorate’s functions and ensure the autonomy of the art institutions. We do not need further investigations to paralyze the basic principles and values of cultural management. I do not see the need for a new investigation into the question of sharing the Council of Culture with a view to bringing together directorate functions in a new directorate, where the Culture Tank is also included. Norwegian Culture Councils have been thoroughly reviewed twice before, by a selection of two different governments. Both studies discussed the question that representatives are promoting the sharing of Norwegian Cultural Councils into two functions, and both studies did not recommend sharing the Council of Culture. A fragmentation will weaken the professional environment, and the combination of arm's length distance tasks and directorate tasks will maximise the use of competences and resources. The arm's length principle is the backbone of Norwegian cultural management. The Ministry assesses the possibility of legislating this principle in the context of the ongoing revision of the Culture Act. The representatives believe that a new assessment of the Culture Council must be seen in the context of the Culture Tank and the Cultural School Bag – we have had that debate earlier today. In the work on the news of art and culture for, with and by children and young people, the department went out and asked for input. On that basis, we have concluded that both the Norwegian Cultural Council and the Cultural Tank are central bodies for the development of art and culture for children and young people. The cultural sector and cultural policy can provide not only art and culture for children and young people. We are dependent on commitment cooperation with other sectors, especially the education sector. The government will therefore stick to the Culture idea because we need a central body that can be a bridge builder and help to ensure this cooperation. The government does not want to create new structures, but rather strengthen the structures already in place regionally and locally, in cooperation with the county councils and municipalities.

ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e5059	A few moments in history, we never forget. March 27, 1980, was such a moment. That a platform in the North Sea had overturned was a totally unreal news to get. I was 21 years old, and my relationship with platforms was created by reports and exciting stories about the adventure that happened out there in the North Sea – many new jobs, and what that meant for our country. But it was really and changed the life of many people in a moment: leaving who lost one of theirs, survivors who were through traumatic moments, and all around these individuals and families who were affected by this at the time and until today. To hit the story again the first time in the spring of 2019 and then work on it again in the spring of 2021 has been demanding. I would like to thank the Committee on very good cooperation in this matter. I would also like to thank the Kielland network for the dialogue we have had these years. The Kielland network has done a considerable amount of voluntary work and has communicated its thoughts and wishes to us in a good way in the Committee on Control and Constitutional Affairs. The Network’s clear message and objectives when they contacted the Committee in 2019 were to re-examine because they had too many unanswered questions after the work done by the original investigation commission. They were also clear that, in their opinion, there were both uncertainties and unclear processes in the work that had been done, and the network also pointed out the poor work that was done around the survivors and left behind after the accident. In 2019 the Supervisory and Constituent Committee, following careful assessments, concluded that a new Landfill Investigation Commission was not right, but chose to present a proposal to the Parliament to ask the Riksaudition to investigate how the authorities have taken care of their responsibilities in relation to the Alexander L. Kielland accident with regard to the following three points: investigation and clarification of the causes of the Alexander L. Kielland accident, and the adequacy of the investigation on the responsibilities of the accident, the follow-up to recommendations from the investigation work, including the implementation of measures to prevent and prevent new accidents, and the follow-up of the survivors and survivors. The National Audit Office delivered its report to Stortinget on 9 March 2021. As usual, the National Audit Office has done a thorough and good job, and I would like to stress the competence and experience of the National Audit Office in looking at how management follows up matters, and the consequences of any deficiencies. The Court of Auditors summarised five conclusions: there is no basis for carrying out a new investigation of the Alexander L. Kielland accident. The authorities did a thorough job of clarifying the causes of the accident, but some weaknesses may have contributed to a weakening of confidence in the investigation. The responsibilities surrounding the accident were not fully investigated. The authorities have followed up the recommendations of the review. The survivors did not receive follow-up, and the follow-up of the survivors was not good enough. The National Audit Office has given the Kielland network the right to see that there were weaknesses in the Commission’s work, and that this may have contributed to a weakening of its confidence in the investigation, among others. the involvement of persons and environments in the investigation which could be called into question for reasons of stability. The Court of Auditors considers that this has not, however, affected the outcome of the investigation. The National Audit Office further notes in the report that the investigation commission was poor at communicating with the affected en route, and that the production of the Commission’s work is upresis and sometimes difficult to understand. The National Audit Service also grants the Kielland network the right to have the responsibilities of the accident not fully clarified. It has been pointed out that the French report could contribute to clarification if it was translated. The National Auditorate has also done that job and states that there are many coincidental findings in the two reports, although the responsibility for any errors is not shared equally. What about further investigation to find more answers? The National Audit Office considers that there is no basis for a re-examination, and this is justified by the fact that it is not likely that significant new information could be generated so long after the accident. Large parts of the technical evidence are gone, and even new analyses and simulations will be difficult to obtain more reliable information than the basis of 1980. It will also be difficult to find reliable and accurate information on responsibilities. The study has also shown the lack of both routines and control in a growing sector: lack of safety training, lack of procedures for recording incidents and no procedures for controlling closed and open hatches and doors. There is no doubt that this also made the Commission’s investigations more difficult. Today, there is a completely different regime, among other things. on the basis of lessons learned from this accident – that’s good. In summary, the Riksrevision states that the authorities did a thorough job of clarifying the causes of the accident, and that the recommendations of the investigation have been followed up in retrospect. The most serious thing – and today almost incomprehensible – is that survivors and survivors were not followed up well enough. The survivors were naturally given medical help after being rescued, but those who did not need this were sent home without any kind of psychiatric assistance in the emergency phase. Those who came to the hospital received such help. However, the lessons learned after investigating the accident in retrospect have been important for the follow-up that is taking place today following major accidents. Even more incomprehensible and serious is the fact that the authorities opted for the follow-up of the survivors, despite the fact that the medical profession pointed out the need for this. We've learned from this today, but it's a skinny comfort for those who didn't get help. In the vote, SV promotes a proposal for further studies, which they themselves will explain. Furthermore, a Joint Committee is in line with the conclusions of the National Audit Office. The Committee also wishes to show respect for and to contribute to the thorough and important work of the Kielland network. It is therefore an overall committee that promotes the four draft decisions of the opinion. The most important thing is I, where the Storting strongly regrets the poor follow-up of survivors and left behind after the accident in April 1980. This is also followed up in II, by asking the government to ensure that a study is carried out in cooperation with the Kielland network in order to gain knowledge of the consequences of the lack of follow-up. Furthermore, in III, the government is asking the Stortinget to engage in dialogue with the Kielland network to work to gather the network's collected material and to contribute to documentation. A lot is done, and something has to be done. The Committee hopes that the Kielland network and the government in good dialogue can find out. ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e5163	The Kielland accident is the biggest industrial accident in Norway's history. 123 people died, 89 were saved, and even more relatives lost some of their closest ones in the accident. As the National Audit Office's investigation shows, the accident is still not thoroughly investigated, so Red supports SV's proposal for a new investigation. Firstly, the Riksrevision reveals that there are deficiencies in the 1981 review. Secondly, significant and important progress has been made since then to provide new information on the causes of the accident, both the direct causes and the more underlying causes of management and organisation. Thirdly, there are still time witnesses who have not been questioned. Fourthly – and that may be the most important thing – we have a commitment to the survivors to do everything we can to get the facts on the table about the accident that hit them, so that they can finally know the truth, put the doubt behind them and move on. In addition, we are not yet sure whether we know the full and complete truth about the role of the Norwegian Veritas, who was responsible for inspection and control, may have had, directly or indirectly, in the Commission which carried out the 1981 review. What is certain, on the other hand, is that when the Riks revision reveals significant shortcomings in the previous review, and more than 80 pst. Of the survivors, they say they do not trust that investigation, we believe that the Storting should take hold. The message from the survivors and the survivors, from NITO, from Tekna, from the leaders and also from the oil trade union SAFE is clear: the Kielland accident must be re-examined. We believe that the Storting must ensure a new study in which we apply new skills and technologies, both survivors and workers' representatives, must be represented, and, in particular, we must have the greatest possible transparency about the study. We also agree with the requirement that the Kielland network and the trade unions should be involved in creating a mandate for such a new study. Those who have been sent out to the North Sea under difficult and dangerous weather conditions have provided Norway with huge oil and gas revenues, but many of them have paid a very high price, not least the North Sea divers and the survivors after the Alexander L. Kielland accident. Whereas there is a particular responsibility on the State to carry out thorough investigations in the event of major accidents, both in the interests of the persons concerned and not least in order to prevent the occurrence of new accidents; 41 years after the Kielland accident, the Norwegian State has not provided an adequate investigation into how and why this disaster occurred. The survivors and survivors say they are struggling to move on as long as there is still doubt that the truth of the accident has come to a close. Nor should the Storting put the matter behind us and move on until we have done everything in our power to get all the facts on the table. ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e5216	I would also like to begin by expressing my condolences to the survivors and leaving behind the Alexander L. Kielland accident on March 27, 1980. The accident was a disaster in which 123 people died. Many individuals and families were hit hard. The National Audit Office's report is an important contribution to the history of the accident and its follow-up. We must recognise that the petroleum industry naturally has a potential for major accidents. Safety is fresh and requires continuous follow-up and improvement. The government's ambition is that Norwegian petroleum activities will be world leaders in HMS. This ambition was anchored in Stortinget through the treatment of Meldning. St. 12 for 2017-2018 on HMS in the petroleum industry. It is important to say that the current HMS regime in the petroleum industry has been developed on the basis of just the historical experience we have since the start of Norwegian petroleum activities. Different events and investigations as well as extensive dialogue with the parties have been very important for development. It was, in particular, the Alexander L. Kielland accident that, understandably and correctly, led to extensive changes to both the legislation and the supervisory system in Norway. Following the accident, the authorities agreed on a new control regime clarifying the division of responsibilities between authorities. Important clarifications according to the roles and responsibilities of the authorities came with the Petroleum Act in 1985. The responsibility of the owner and operator was made clear, and the Oil Directorate, later the Petroleum Authority, was given overall responsibility for safety oversight of the base. The overall authority's responsibility is now with the Petroleum Authority. The Riksrevision concludes that the authorities have followed up the recommendations of the investigation following the Kielland accident and that there has been a significant improvement in the safety of mobile devices after the accident – although the Riksrevision, as has been said here, also points to areas where the authorities and we as society have not followed up. The development of the current Norwegian-socket safety regime has been a long-term development. From a legislative framework that specified in detail the requirements for structures, technical equipment and operations, from the 1980s, a regulatory framework with a high level of functional requirements was developed that emphasises the responsibility of actors to set criteria for, and follow up, their own activities. Supervision of the activities, which at the start-up stage were characterised by a high degree of detailed control by the authorities, has gradually developed to focus on the accountability of actors and the systematic follow-up of their own activities. Such an approximation of powers is important in an industry characterised by a high level of competence and very rapid technological development. This regime, with functional requirements and clear accountability of industry, a system-based monitoring of the emphasis on two- and three-party cooperation, is well adapted to the petroleum industry. There is also a broad consensus that the HMS regime has contributed over time to the positive development of the HMS level in the petroleum industry and that the safety level in the industry is evenly high above. Why do I spend time on this? Well, because this accident was so huge, and the consequences were so big that it's also important in retrospect that we learned the crucial thing about the development of HMS standards and the safety of the socket – not because we can guard against accidents, but because we can also think in this area that we should do everything we can to ensure that it doesn't happen similarly again. It's also an important legacy after the Alexander L. Kielland accident. It also means that the established HMS regime will continue to be important. It's a high risk potential and rapid development, it's important that we take care of good party cooperation while also thinking about how to further develop this for the coming years.
