ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e5509	I promise I won't use a block! Prop. 180 S for 2020-2021 concerns consent to participation in a decision of the EEA Committee on the inclusion in the EEA Agreement of a Regulation on the EU Civil Protection Mechanism. The proposal proposes to continue Norway’s long-standing cooperation with the EU in civil protection. The system has a duration from 2021 to 2027. The consent of the majority to participate is necessary in accordance with the second paragraph of Grundlova § 26. The proposition is based on the latest draft Regulation 2020/0097 (COD), which is based mainly on the 2019 Regulation, but has been amended and strengthened, etc. in the light of the wide-ranging impact of the ongoing covid-19 pandemic on a number of European countries. The aim of the scheme is to facilitate broad cooperation in the area of social security and preparedness in Europe. The changes that follow the latest draft regulation make changes in how the EU supports, coordinates and complements its efforts in the field of preparedness and crisis management to ensure the prevention, preparedness and crisis management of natural and man-made disasters for its own citizens in and outside Europe. The EU Civil Protection Mechanism represents an important safety net for Norway, especially for situations that rarely occur but have considerable consequences, and which are difficult to manage by one country alone. Through the scheme, Norway has undertaken to establish an air ambulance for the transport of patients with serious and contagious diseases as a capacity in the European Resource Pool RescEU. The air ambulance is based in Norway, and Norway can use its capacity when not in use in the EU. Norway's participation is also important for our cooperation with the other Nordic countries on civil protection. Only in the last year has our participation in the scheme given us valuable help, both in connection with the Gjerdrum camp, where a USR team from Gothenburg helped, and for an assisted return trip for about 600 Norwegian citizens abroad at the beginning of the pandemic outbreak. I would like to stress that making national capacities available in the civil protection reserve does not change the fundamental principle of national self-determination in a situation where Norway would need these capacities itself. The system does not replace national response capacities, but is intended to be a necessary reinforcement in the event of major disasters. Finally, I would like to thank the Committee on Cooperation. There is a joint committee behind the setting.

ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e5548	Changes in the Econom Act [[storage of IP addresses, etc.]] The storage time proposed by the government is 12 months — up to 12 months — while the Labour Party, the Centre Party and SV want to limit it to six months. It's an interesting discussion, and what I notice is that you essentially refer to privacy. Then I think it's important to understand what IP addresses really are. An IP address is an Internet Protocol address. It's kind of, to put it simply, the phone number you have when you operate on the Internet. In the old days, we had something called phone catalogues. Then you could look up – that’s actually true – and then you could find which phone number was attached to which person. The only thing that we do here is that they actually have to physically store who this address, that is, the IP address, is for a limited period of time, whether it's for six months, that the minority wants, or for twelve months, that the majority of people in this room want. What is at least not the case is that this, with all the misunderstandings, is a starting point for monitoring. It's not about storing traffic data. There is no way to store metadata and other type of information. The only thing the police want is that they get the information on the day they get a warning from, for example. The FBI said a Norwegian user was in a pedophilia, etc. Then you can go in and connect the IP address you get from the FBI to a person, which you can then catch. That's what this is all about. That is why this is an important matter, and fortunately it gets the majority today.

The ultimate consequence of this is less obvious abuse of children.

ParlaMint-NO_2021-06-07.ana.ud218e5863	Criminal gangs represent a significant challenge. Through violence, threats and other crime, these contribute to the creation of population insecurity. We have no place for criminal gangs in our society. In recent years, we have seen a negative trend in recrimination. Very young people are recruited to the rest of the world. The recruiters are in danger of getting into a criminal racetrack, with all the negative consequences that this could entail. The challenges must be met with a number of instruments and measures — both prevention and punishment. I am very pleased that an overall judicial committee has supported the government's proposal for new provisions in the Criminal Procedures Act and the Criminal Code, aimed at participation in and recruitment to criminal associations. The new rules involve two steps of criminalisation. First, a criminal association must be banned by law. Then there is the right to punish those who participate in or recruit for membership of such associations. Compared to a traditional criminal offence, this model provides a more precise indication of the criminal offence area. It increases predictability for individuals and spaces in order to make somewhat less stringent demands on the forms of participation affected by the criminal offence. The new rules may make it less attractive for international criminal organisations to establish subgroups in Norway. Another possible consequence is that the criminal associations choose to be less visible in the public. Although this does not in itself solve the challenges of recriminalism, it can have a positive impact on society’s sense of security and make it more difficult for groups to recruit new members. The criminalisation of participation may also enable the victims to be affected. Today, it is a challenge that the leadership of criminals' gangs deliberately distance themselves from the concrete criminal acts. The proposal addresses established associations that commit serious crime and spread fear in the community. We propose that participation in and recruitment to such associations be punished by imprisonment up to three years. In addition to other measures, the new rules could be a valuable tool in combating recriminalism.
